<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that the density-one triangular lattice minimizes the lower energy per particle for every nonnegative completely monotone function of squared distance, among all locally finite planar configurations of centered disk density one. The comparison includes infinite energies. The proof constructs sharp Gaussian Fourier minorants using an atomic interpolation certificate.

- **claim-universal-energy-minimum** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-universal)): For every smooth nonnegative completely monotone function $g$ on $(0,\infty)$ and every locally finite planar set $\mathcal C$ of centered disk density one, $$E_g(\mathcal C)\ge \sum_{a\in A\setminus\{0\}}g(\|a\|^2)=E_g(A).$$ The comparison and the equality are in the extended nonnegative reals.
  - Explanation: The triangular lattice of covolume one minimizes the asymptotic lower energy per particle among all locally finite planar configurations of centered disk density one, for every smooth completely monotone potential of squared distance. The comparison holds in the extended nonnegative reals.
- **claim-sharp-gaussian-minorants** (construction, [source](#stmt-thm-gaussian)): For every $\alpha>0$ there is a real radial Schwartz function $f_\alpha:\mathbb R^2\to\mathbb R$ such that $$f_\alpha(x)\le k_\alpha(x),\qquad \widehat f_\alpha(\xi)\ge0 \quad(x,\xi\in\mathbb R^2),$$ and $$f_\alpha(a)=k_\alpha(a)\quad(a\in A\setminus\{0\}),\qquad \widehat f_\alpha(w)=0\quad(w\in A^*\setminus\{0\}).$$ For $\alpha\ge1$, the construction uses two finite $20$-by-$20$ interpolation blocks and an absolutely summable infinite correction.
  - Explanation: For each positive Gaussian parameter there is a radial Schwartz minorant with nonnegative Fourier transform that touches the Gaussian at nonzero lattice points and whose transform vanishes at nonzero dual lattice points.
- **claim-reciprocal-gaussian-parameters** (theorem, [source](#stmt-energy-reciprocal)): If <a href="#energy:input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:input">[energy:input]</a> holds for every $\alpha\ge1$, it holds for every $\alpha>0$. For each of these functions, $$\widehat f_\alpha(0)-f_\alpha(0) =\sum_{a\in A\setminus\{0\}}e^{-\pi\alpha\|a\|^2}. \label{energy:poisson}$$
  - Explanation: Gaussian Fourier duality together with the quarter-turn relation between the lattice and its dual transfers the minorant construction from parameters at least one to all positive parameters, and gives the Poisson-summation identity for the lattice sum.
- **claim-fourier-mass-near-zero** (theorem, [source](#stmt-energy-low-frequency)): For every fixed $\varepsilon>0$, the exponential sum $M_R(\xi)=\sum_{x\in\mathcal C_R}e^{2\pi i x\cdot\xi}$ satisfies $$\liminf_{R\to\infty}\frac1{N_R} \int_{\|\xi\|\le\varepsilon}\|M_R(\xi)\|^2\,d\xi\ge1. \label{energy:fourier-mass}$$
  - Explanation: Using only the centered disk count, the low-frequency Fourier mass of the exponential sum of a configuration is asymptotically at least the number of points.
- **claim-density-only-lp-bound** (theorem, [source](#stmt-energy-lp)): Let $f$ be a real even Schwartz function on $\mathbb R^2$ with $\widehat f\ge0$, and let $\mathcal C$ be locally finite with $N_R/(\pi R^2)\to1$. Then $$\liminf_{R\to\infty}\frac1{N_R} \sum_{\substack{x,y\in\mathcal C_R\\x\ne y}}f(x-y) \ge\widehat f(0)-f(0). \label{energy:lp-bound}$$ The same lower bound holds if the summands are replaced by $\Phi(x-y)$, where $\Phi(z)\ge f(z)$ for every $z\ne0$.
  - Explanation: For a real even Schwartz function with nonnegative transform, the off-diagonal energy per particle of any centered-density-one configuration is bounded below by the transform at zero minus the function at zero; the bound persists for any larger pair interaction off the diagonal.
- **claim-positive-laplace-representation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-energy-laplace)): Suppose $g:(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)$ is smooth and $(-1)^j g^{(j)}(s)\ge0$ for every integer $j\ge0$ and every $s>0$. There is a positive locally finite Borel measure $\nu$ on $[0,\infty)$ such that $$g(s)=\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{-st}\,d\nu(t)\qquad(s>0). \label{energy:laplace-representation}$$ The integral is finite for every $s>0$; the mass $\nu([0,\infty))$ need not be finite.
  - Explanation: A completely monotone function on the open half-line is a Laplace integral of a positive locally finite Borel measure that may have infinite total mass and an atom at zero.
- **claim-finite-certificate** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-finite-certificate)): The following strict inequalities hold for the constants and matrices defined above.* 1. *The pivots of diagonal-order Gauss–Jordan elimination on $I-R$ and $I+R$ have absolute values between $0.12$ and $1.74$. Moreover, $$\begin{gathered} \\|(I-R)^{-1}\\|<16.16<17,\qquad \\|(I+R)^{-1}\\|<3.89<5,\\ \sup_{e\in\mathcal E}\\|A_1e\\|<2.58,\qquad \sup_{e\in\mathcal E}\\|A_2e\\|<2.80. \end{gathered}$$* 2. *The node quantities satisfy $0.157<Q_n<40$ and $\|D_n\|<2.83$ for every residue node, and $\min_{n=16,19,21}Q_n>16$. For successive gaps of one period, starting with $(0,1)$, the midpoint values of $P(s)/(s-m)^2$, with $m$ either endpoint, exceed respectively $$\begin{gathered} 0.165,\ 0.120,\ 0.054,\ 0.482,\ 0.656,\ 1.11,\ 0.337,\\ 3.13,\ 9.26,\ 8.19,\ 19.08,\ 2.34,\ 0.706,\ 6.80,\ 14.1. \end{gathered}$$* 3. *For the jet envelope sums $\Psi_{N,k,v}(S)$ just defined, including geometric summation over all tail periods, the bounds are $$\begin{array}{c\|c\|c\|c\|c} N&S&k&v&\text{upper bound}\\\hline f&W&h&H&335\\ T&M&H&h&7\cdot10^{-15}\\ T&W&h&h&2.3\cdot10^{-8}\\ T&V&h&h&1.27\cdot10^{-7} \end{array}$$ Here $\\|W\\|<119$, and the repetition bound is $$\frac{1+\pi\eta}{e^{36\pi\eta}-1}<2.5\cdot10^{-10}.$$ The omitted target envelope sums over $f\cap[9,21]$ and $T$ are less than $8.1\cdot10^{-8}$ and $9.2\cdot10^{-29}$ respectively.* 4. *The raw curvature envelope sums defined above have upper bounds $$\begin{array}{c\|rrrrr} s&0&1&2&14.5&23\\\hline L&1498&45.1&13.8&0.00458&0.000022\\ \text{transformed constant}&13530&151&31&0.00122&0.000004\\ T&26500&1160&800&800&800 \end{array}$$ The divided Taylor remainder envelope is less than $0.00161$ when $m=1$ and less than $0.0000075$ at every other listed $m$, for both listed displacements $y$.* 5. *For every $e\in\mathcal E$ and $0\leq\tau\leq1$, the following polynomials have the stated strict lower bounds: $$\begin{array}{c\|c\|c} m,y&\text{polynomial}&\text{lower bound}\\\hline 1,-1&T_2&0.174\\ 1,-1&Q_1/(2Z)-T_1&0.057\\ 1,1&T_2&0.011\\ 3,\ \text{either}&0.8Q_1w-T_1&0.00055\\ 3,\ \text{either}&T_2&0.00097\\ 4,\ \text{either}&-T_1&0.00047\\ 4,\ \text{either}&T_2&0.00097\\ 7,\ \text{either}&-T_1,\ T_2&0.0028\\ 9,\ \text{either}&-T_1,\ T_2&0.0039\\ 12,13,\ \text{either}&-T_1,\ T_2&0.0020 \end{array} \label{eq:finite-sign-tests}$$ For $m=y=1$, put $v=\tau$ and $B_0=v^2$, $B_1=v^2+2Zv$, $B_2=v^2+4Zv+6Z^2$. Then $$\begin{array}{c\|c} \text{polynomial}&\text{lower bound}\\\hline Q_1+vA&0.046\\ Q_1(v+Z)+(2B_1A+B_0D)/3&0.056\\ Q_1(v+2Z)+(B_2A+2B_1D)/3&0.024\\ Q_1(v+3Z)+B_2D&0.027 \end{array} \label{eq:exceptional-tests}$$ This last table holds uniformly for all allowed $w,\Delta,r,p$ and all pairs $(a,u)$ with $0\le u\le U_*$ and $0\le a\le Zu$.
  - Explanation: A finite list of strict inequalities, certified by outward-rounded interval arithmetic over the whole parameter polytope, bounds pivots, inverse norms, node quantities, envelopes and polynomial lower bounds used by the infinite correction and the sign argument.
- **claim-exact-interpolation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-exact-interpolation)): For every $\alpha\ge1$, let $z$ and $e$ be its Gaussian parameter and data vector defined in Sections <a href="#sec:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:construction">3</a> and <a href="#sec:finite-certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite-certificate">4</a>. Retain the fixed coefficients $c_{i,0},d_{i,0},C_i$ in <a href="#analytic:input-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="analytic:input-functions">[analytic:input-functions]</a>. There are unique real coefficient lists $$u_i\in\mathbb R^{20},\qquad w_i\in\ell^1(T;\mathbb R^2)\qquad(i=1,2),$$ indexed respectively by the pairs $(c_{i,n},d_{i,n})$ on $f$ and $T$, for which the functions $J_i=e^{\pi hs}F_i$ from that construction satisfy $$J_1(n)=G_z(n),\quad J_1'(n)=G_z'(n),\qquad J_2(n)=J_2'(n)=0\qquad(n\in\mathcal N).$$ Relative to the finite coefficient lists $A_i e$, they obey $$\\|w_i\\|_1<3\cdot10^{-9},\qquad \\|u_i-(A_i e)_f\\|_1<10^{-5}\qquad(i=1,2). \label{analytic:individual-errors}$$ The resulting $F_i$ are real radial Schwartz functions with $\widehat F_1=F_2$. Uniqueness concerns these coefficient lists with the prescribed fixed columns, not all radial Schwartz functions with the same interpolation data.
  - Explanation: For every parameter at least one there are unique finite and summable tail coefficient lists whose functions satisfy value-and-derivative interpolation at all nodes exactly, with small errors relative to the finite approximation, and form a Fourier pair of radial Schwartz functions.
- **claim-curvature-envelopes** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-curvature-envelopes)): Suppose that on each side the finite pole coefficients, indexed by $L$, have norm at most $a_L$, and the tail coefficients have norm at most $a_T$. The contribution of these coefficients to either $J_i$ has half its absolute second derivative bounded by $a_L\mathcal L(s_0)+a_T\mathcal T(s_0)$ for every $s\ge s_0$, where $$\label{eq:curvature-envelope-table} \begin{array}{c\|rrrrr} s_0&0&1&2&14.5&23\\\hline \mathcal L(s_0)&1510&46&14&.00465&.000023\\ \mathcal T(s_0)&26500&1160&800&800&800 \end{array}$$ The transformed constant column has half its absolute second derivative at most $.002$ per unit coefficient on $s\ge14.5$.
  - Explanation: Half the absolute second derivative of the pole-column contributions is bounded uniformly beyond each listed radius by the finite and tail coefficient norms weighted by tabulated envelopes.
- **claim-deleted-jet-accuracy** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-deleted-jets)): Let $T_i(\tau)$ be the degree-$20$ Taylor polynomial <a href="#eq:finite-taylor-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-taylor-polynomial">[eq:finite-taylor-polynomial]</a> for $U_m[\widetilde J_i](y\tau)$. Uniformly for the cells in <a href="#eq:near-cells" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:near-cells">[eq:near-cells]</a> and $0\le\tau\le1$, $$\label{eq:deleted-jet-error} \|U_m[J_i](y\tau)-T_i(\tau)\|< \begin{cases} .018,&m=1,\ y=-1,\\ .003,&m=1,\ y=1,\\ .00016,&m\ge3. \end{cases}$$
  - Explanation: After deleting value and derivative at a node, the exact divided remainder differs from the degree-twenty Taylor polynomial of the finite approximation by a small tabulated amount on each near cell.
- **claim-gaussian-divided-difference** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-gaussian-divided-difference)): For $G(s)=Q_1ze^{(1-s)/z}$, $z>0$, and every $m,v$, $$\label{eq:gaussian-divided-difference} U_m[G](v)\ge Q_1e^{(1-m)/z} \begin{cases} (2z)^{-1},&v\le0,\\[2pt] \displaystyle\frac{3z+v}{6z^2+4zv+v^2},&v\ge0. \end{cases}$$ Both bounds have their continuous meaning at $v=0$.
  - Explanation: The Gaussian target has an explicit lower bound for its second-order divided remainder, from convexity for negative displacement and a rational envelope for positive displacement.
- **claim-global-signs** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-global-signs)): The exact interpolation functions satisfy $$J_1(s)\le G(s),\qquad J_2(s)\ge0\qquad(s\ge0).$$
  - Explanation: The exact interpolation functions stay below the Gaussian target on the first and above zero on the second over the entire half-line, combining finite polynomial tests, error bounds and a sine-product barrier.
- **claim-sine-product-lower-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-sine-barrier)): If $m$ is a nearest zero of $P$ to $s$ and $s\ne m$, then $$P(s)>.054(s-m)^2.$$ For $14.5\le s\le23$, choose a nearest node from $\{16,19,21\}$; if $s\ne m$, then $P(s)>3(s-m)^2$.
  - Explanation: The periodic sine product is bounded below by a quadratic in the distance to its nearest zero, with a stronger constant on an intermediate range.

  T&W&h&h&2.3\\cdot10^{-8}\\\\\n     T&V&h&h&1.27\\cdot10^{-7}\n    \\end{array}$$ Here $\\|W\\|<119$, and the repetition bound is $$\\frac{1+\\pi\\eta}{e^{36\\pi\\eta}-1}<2.5\\cdot10^{-10}.$$ The omitted target envelope sums over $f\\cap[9,21]$ and $T$ are less than $8.1\\cdot10^{-8}$ and $9.2\\cdot10^{-29}$ respectively.*\n\n4.  *The raw curvature envelope sums defined above have upper bounds $$\\begin{array}{c|rrrrr}\n     s&0&1&2&14.5&23\\\\\\hline\n     L&1498&45.1&13.8&0.00458&0.000022\\\\\n     \\text{transformed constant}&13530&151&31&0.00122&0.000004\\\\\n     T&26500&1160&800&800&800\n    \\end{array}$$ The divided Taylor remainder envelope is less than $0.00161$ when $m=1$ and less than $0.0000075$ at every other listed $m$, for both listed displacements $y$.*\n\n\n\n5.  *For every $e\\in\\mathcal E$ and $0\\leq\\tau\\leq1$, the following polynomials have the stated strict lower bounds: $$\\begin{array}{c|c|c}\n     m,y&\\text{polynomial}&\\text{lower bound}\\\\\\hline\n     1,-1&T_2&0.174\\\\\n     1,-1&Q_1/(2Z)-T_1&0.057\\\\\n     1,1&T_2&0.011\\\\\n     3,\\ \\text{either}&0.8Q_1w-T_1&0.00055\\\\\n     3,\\ \\text{either}&T_2&0.00097\\\\\n     4,\\ \\text{either}&-T_1&0.00047\\\\\n     4,\\ \\text{either}&T_2&0.00097\\\\\n     7,\\ \\text{either}&-T_1,\\ T_2&0.0028\\\\\n     9,\\ \\text{either}&-T_1,\\ T_2&0.0039\\\\\n     12,13,\\ \\text{either}&-T_1,\\ T_2&0.0020\n    \\end{array}\n     \\label{eq:finite-sign-tests}$$ For $m=y=1$, put $v=\\tau$ and $B_0=v^2$, $B_1=v^2+2Zv$, $B_2=v^2+4Zv+6Z^2$. Then $$\\begin{array}{c|c}\n     \\text{polynomial}&\\text{lower bound}\\\\\\hline\n     Q_1+vA&0.046\\\\\n     Q_1(v+Z)+(2B_1A+B_0D)/3&0.056\\\\\n     Q_1(v+2Z)+(B_2A+2B_1D)/3&0.024\\\\\n     Q_1(v+3Z)+B_2D&0.027\n    \\end{array}\n     \\label{eq:exceptional-tests}$$ This last table holds uniformly for all allowed $w,\\Delta,r,p$ and all pairs $(a,u)$ with $0\\le u\\le U_*$ and $0\\le a\\le Zu$.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **centered disk density one** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A locally finite set $\mathcal C\subset\mathbb R^2$ has *centered disk density one* if $N_R/(\pi R^2)\to1$, where $N_R=\#(\mathcal C\cap B_R)$.
- **completely monotone function** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A smooth function $g:(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)$ is *completely monotone* if $$(-1)^j g^{(j)}(t)\ge0\qquad(j=0,1,2,\ldots,\ t>0).$$
- **triangular lattice A** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Put $b=\sqrt3/2$ and let $$A=b^{-1/2}\{m(1,0)+n(1/2,b):m,n\in\mathbb Z\}$$ be the triangular lattice of covolume one.
- **closed disk B_R** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Write $B_R$ for the closed disk of radius $R$ centered at the origin.
- **Gaussian kernel k_alpha** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Write $k_\alpha(x)=e^{-\pi\alpha\|x\|^2}$ for $\alpha>0$.
- **conjugate symmetric measure** ([source](#pm-h-11)): A finite complex measure $\mu$ on $[-5/6,5/6]$ will be called conjugate symmetric if reflection takes it to its complex conjugate.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-reciprocal** [claim](#stmt-energy-reciprocal) / [proof](#proof-stmt-energy-reciprocal): Gaussian Fourier duality and quarter-turn lattice symmetry, then periodization and Poisson summation. -> minorants for all positive parameters and the lattice-sum identity
- **step-low-frequency** [claim](#stmt-energy-low-frequency) / [proof](#proof-stmt-energy-low-frequency): Smooth cutoff of the disk, Fourier inversion, Cauchy-Schwarz and Plancherel. -> lower bound on low-frequency Fourier mass
- **step-density-lp** [claim](#stmt-energy-lp) / [proof](#proof-stmt-energy-lp): Exact finite Fourier identity, continuity of the transform at zero, and removal of diagonal terms. -> off-diagonal energy lower bound
- **step-laplace** [claim](#stmt-energy-laplace) / [proof](#proof-stmt-energy-laplace): Taylor expansion with remainder comparison, discrete approximating measures and a compactness argument. -> positive Laplace representation
- **step-universal-energy** [claim](#stmt-thm-universal) / [proof](#proof-stmt-energy-laplace-2): Mix the Gaussian energy inequalities against the Laplace measure using Fatou and Tonelli, then treat the lattice limit. -> universal energy minimum
- **step-finite-certificate** [claim](#stmt-lem-finite-certificate) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-finite-certificate): Outward-rounded interval arithmetic, Gauss-Jordan elimination and Bernstein row minima over the polytope. -> finite inequalities
- **step-exact-interpolation** [claim](#stmt-prop-exact-interpolation) / [proof](#analytic:schur-tail): Split into sum and difference systems, Schur elimination of finite variables and Neumann series on the tail space. -> exact interpolation lists with error bounds
- **step-curvature-envelopes** [claim](#stmt-lem-curvature-envelopes) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-curvature-envelopes): Differentiate the waves twice and bound by frequency extrema using the finite endpoint evaluations. -> curvature envelope table
- **step-deleted-jets** [claim](#stmt-lem-deleted-jets) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-deleted-jets): Wave expansion with geometric remainder plus curvature bounds on the correction. -> deleted-jet accuracy bounds
- **step-gaussian-divided-difference** [claim](#stmt-lem-gaussian-divided-difference) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-gaussian-divided-difference): Convexity for negative displacement; an auxiliary function with nonnegative second derivative for positive displacement. -> lower bounds on the divided remainder of the target
- **step-sine-barrier** [claim](#stmt-lem-sine-barrier) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-sine-barrier): Log-concavity of the divided sine product on half-gaps and midpoint lower bounds. -> quadratic lower bound for the sine product
- **step-global-signs** [claim](#stmt-prop-global-signs) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-global-signs): Deleted-jet comparison on near cells with Bernstein tests, then sine-product domination of curvature farther out. -> global sign inequalities
- **step-gaussian-minorants** [claim](#stmt-thm-gaussian) / [proof](#pm-h-25): Normalize the first interpolation function and combine with the reciprocal-parameter extension. -> sharp Gaussian minorants for all positive parameters

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-residue-set-mod-36**: The residues modulo 36 taken by the form m^2+mn+n^2 over integer pairs are exactly the fifteen residues of the displayed set I, so every nonzero lattice squared-radius coordinate lies in the interpolation node set.
- **inv-crt-combination**: Combining the admissible residues modulo 4 with those modulo 9 by the Chinese remainder theorem yields exactly the set I.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-superset-is-shells**: Treat every residue in the node set as the squared radius of an actual lattice shell. -> Some nodes need not be realised by any lattice point; the interpolation is imposed on a superset.
- **anti-classify-minimisers**: Read the residue and certificate machinery as classifying all minimising configurations. -> The result identifies the minimum value only; uniqueness of minimisers is not established.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The residue-set claim for the quadratic form is a finite computation over one period; the program enumerates integer pairs in a symmetric window and reports the set of residues modulo 36.

```python
# INPUTS: one integer n >= 0 on stdin (window half-width for m and k)
# PRECONDITIONS: n is a non-negative integer
# OUTPUTS: sorted distinct residues of m*m+m*k+k*k mod 36 for m,k in [-n,n], comma separated
# TIME: O(n^2) pair evaluations
# SPACE: O(1) beyond the residue set (at most 36 entries)
# ARITHMETIC: exact Python integers
# FAILURE: malformed input raises an exception and the run exits non-zero
import sys
n = int(sys.stdin.read().split()[0])
res = set()
for m in range(-n, n + 1):
    for k in range(-n, n + 1):
        res.add((m*m + m*k + k*k) % 36)
print(",".join(str(x) for x in sorted(res)))
```

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves a mathematical comparison of energies. It specifies no program, interface or data format a software module could implement from the statements alone. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The energy comparison concerns infinite planar configurations of centered disk density one. A finite simulated cluster or non-unit density arrangement does not satisfy the hypothesis. |
| tools | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | The appendix describes a finite certificate-checking recipe: outward-rounded interval arithmetic and Bernstein row minima over a parameter polytope. A verification tool could follow it, but only for this specific certificate. |
| algorithms | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | The finite part of the proof is an explicit computation: diagonal-order Gauss-Jordan elimination on two twenty-by-twenty matrices, with interval enclosures of the inverse norms. This is adaptable to a verifier, but the paper states no complexity analysis and the main theorems are existential. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No data structure is introduced or analysed. Point sets appear only as locally finite subsets of the plane in an analytic argument. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No hardware, parallel evaluation scheme or numerical kernel is proposed. The only computation is a one-off interval-arithmetic certificate. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing concerns keys, hardness assumptions or protocols. Lattices appear only as the planar triangular lattice in an energy problem. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No learning problem, loss or training procedure occurs. Completely monotone kernels are treated as pair interactions, not as learned models. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The density-only linear programming bound is a proof device giving a lower bound on energy. The paper gives no solver, no feasible-set computation and no algorithmic optimisation of configurations. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No communication, consensus or fault model occurs; the setting is a static infinite planar point set. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A physical reading (ground states of repulsive planar particle systems) is only an interpretation of the energy; the paper states a theorem for completely monotone functions of squared distance and no physical model. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **lim-centered-density-hypothesis** (hypothesis): The energy lower bound is proved only for locally finite planar sets whose centered disk count grows like the disk area. Sets violating this density condition are outside the theorem. Blocked use: ['Applying the bound to finite droplets or clusters', 'Applying the bound to configurations of non-unit asymptotic density']
- **lim-complete-monotonicity** (hypothesis): The potential must be smooth, nonnegative and completely monotone on the open half-line; the Laplace mixing of Gaussian kernels needs this class. Blocked use: ['Applying the theorem to potentials with attractive wells or oscillating sign', 'Applying it to potentials whose derivatives fail to alternate in sign']
- **lim-minimizers-not-classified** (formal_scope): The theorem identifies the minimum value only. It gives no uniqueness of the triangular lattice and no description of other minimizing configurations. Blocked use: ['Claiming that the triangular lattice is the unique ground state', 'Claiming that defective or disordered configurations cannot reach the minimum']
- **lim-no-general-auxiliary** (nonconstructive): Sharp auxiliary functions are constructed only for individual Gaussians. Mixing the energy inequalities yields no sharp Schwartz auxiliary for a general potential. Blocked use: ['Expecting an explicit sharp auxiliary function for an arbitrary completely monotone potential', 'Using the Gaussian construction as a per-potential auxiliary generator']
- **lim-half-range-input** (hypothesis): The reciprocal extension to all positive Gaussian parameters is conditional on the construction for parameters at least one. Without that construction, small parameters are not covered. Blocked use: ['Using the reciprocal extension without the construction for parameters at least one']
- **lim-laplace-compactness** (nonconstructive): The Laplace measure is obtained by a compactness argument along a diagonal subsequence. It may have infinite total mass and an atom at zero, so it is not an explicit finite formula. Blocked use: ['Computing the representing measure by a closed formula', 'Assuming finite total mass of the measure']
- **lim-finite-certificate-polytope** (formal_scope): The finite inequalities are certified for the fixed constants and matrices and over the stated parameter polytope. A change of constants requires re-running the certificate. Blocked use: ['Reusing the certificate with different damping constants', 'Replacing outward-rounded arithmetic by floating-point evaluation']
- **lim-planar-modulo-36** (edge_case): The node set, modulo-36 residues and Fourier scaling are specific to the planar triangular lattice. The paper does not extend them to other dimensions. Blocked use: ['Transplanting the node set to three-dimensional lattices', 'Using the residue set for other quadratic forms']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 090 (`lean/docs/090.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [An atomic certificate for triangular-lattice universal optimality](../../preprints/An-atomic-certificate-for-triangular-lattice-universal-optimality-September-26-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [A sharp Fourier certificate for planar circle packing](../../preprints/A-sharp-Fourier-certificate-for-planar-circle-packing-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization proves that the density-one triangular lattice minimizes lower energy per particle among all locally finite planar configurations of centered-disk density one, for every nonnegative completely monotone function of squared distance. Infinite energies are allowed in the comparison.
>
> It also constructs sharp radial Schwartz minorants for every Gaussian potential: each minorant lies below the Gaussian, has nonnegative real Fourier transform, agrees with the Gaussian at nonzero triangular-lattice points, and vanishes on nonzero dual-lattice points after Fourier transformation. For the stated parameter range, the formalization includes the explicit atomic interpolation construction.
>
> The planar Cohn–Elkies sharpness conjecture asks whether the two-point Fourier method attains the optimal circle-packing density. The formalization constructs a radial Schwartz function $f$ on $\mathbb R^2$ with $\widehat f(0)=1$, $f(0)=2/\sqrt3$, $\widehat f$ real and nonnegative everywhere, and $f(x)\le0$ whenever $\|x\|\ge1$. These are the sharp Fourier certificate conditions yielding density $\pi/(2\sqrt3)$. The separate uniqueness statement for periodic equality cases is outside this selected theorem.

Comparator source `TriangularEnergy.lean` (2500 bytes, sha256 `af51979c7baecd3e9852637bbba29eff96cbb09cec84e15eff7d686f1a770616`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section
open scoped BigOperators Topology ENNReal
open Classical Filter

namespace TriangularUniversal

abbrev Plane := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 2)

def LocallyFinite (C : Set Plane) : Prop :=
  ∀ R : ℝ, (C ∩ Metric.closedBall (0 : Plane) R).Finite

def diskPoints (C : Set Plane) (R : ℝ) : Finset Plane :=
  if h : (C ∩ Metric.closedBall (0 : Plane) R).Finite then h.toFinset else ∅

def diskCount (C : Set Plane) (R : ℝ) : ℕ := (diskPoints C R).card

def DensityOne (C : Set Plane) : Prop :=
  Tendsto (fun R : ℝ => (diskCount C R : ℝ) / (Real.pi * R ^ 2))
    atTop (𝓝 1)

def AdmissiblePotential (g : ℝ → ℝ) : Prop :=
  ContDiffOn ℝ (⊤ : ℕ∞) g (Set.Ioi 0) ∧
  (∀ t : ℝ, 0 < t → 0 ≤ g t) ∧
  ∀ (r : ℕ) (t : ℝ), 0 < t → 0 ≤ (-1 : ℝ) ^ r * iteratedDeriv r g t

def diskEnergy (g : ℝ → ℝ) (C : Set Plane) (R : ℝ) : ℝ≥0∞ :=
  (diskCount C R : ℝ≥0∞)⁻¹ *
    ∑ x ∈ diskPoints C R, ∑ y ∈ (diskPoints C R).erase x,
      ENNReal.ofReal (g (‖x - y‖ ^ 2))

def energy (g : ℝ → ℝ) (C : Set Plane) : ℝ≥0∞ :=
  Filter.liminf (diskEnergy g C) atTop

end TriangularUniversal

namespace AtomicTriangular

abbrev Plane := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 2)

def b : ℝ := Real.sqrt 3 / 2

def triangularPoint (jk : ℤ × ℤ) : Plane :=
  (Real.sqrt b)⁻¹ •
    (EuclideanSpace.single 0 ((jk.1 : ℝ) + (jk.2 : ℝ) / 2) +
      EuclideanSpace.single 1 ((jk.2 : ℝ) * b))

def A : Set Plane := Set.range triangularPoint

abbrev LocallyFinite := TriangularUniversal.LocallyFinite
abbrev diskPoints := TriangularUniversal.diskPoints
abbrev diskCount := TriangularUniversal.diskCount
abbrev DensityOne := TriangularUniversal.DensityOne
abbrev AdmissiblePotential := TriangularUniversal.AdmissiblePotential
abbrev diskEnergy := TriangularUniversal.diskEnergy
abbrev energy := TriangularUniversal.energy

/-- The full nonzero-lattice energy, allowing a divergent series. -/
def latticeEnergy (g : ℝ → ℝ) : ℝ≥0∞ :=
  ∑' a : {x : Plane // x ∈ A ∧ x ≠ 0}, ENNReal.ofReal (g (‖a.val‖ ^ 2))

/-- Universal energy minimality for smooth completely monotone potentials, with attainment. -/
theorem universal_energy_minimum (g : ℝ → ℝ) (C : Set Plane)
    (hg : AdmissiblePotential g) (hC : LocallyFinite C) (hd : DensityOne C) :
    latticeEnergy g ≤ energy g C ∧ latticeEnergy g = energy g A := by
  sorry

end AtomicTriangular
end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that the density-one triangular lattice minimizes the lower energy per particle for every nonnegative completely monotone function of squared distance, among all locally finite planar configurations of centered disk density one. The comparison includes infinite energies. The proof constructs sharp Gaussian Fourier minorants using an atomic interpolation certificate.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Universal optimality asks for one configuration that minimizes energy for an entire family of repulsive interactions. For the triangular lattice, the difficulty is not only to compare different lattices. A competing planar set may have no periods, arbitrarily close pairs, and arbitrarily large counts in small translated disks. We begin by isolating a Fourier comparison that uses only the asymptotic number of points in centered disks, and then construct functions that make this comparison sharp.

Put $b=\sqrt3/2$ and let $$A=b^{-1/2}\{m(1,0)+n(1/2,b):m,n\in\mathbb Z\}$$ be the triangular lattice of covolume one. Write $B_R$ for the closed disk of radius $R$ centered at the origin. A locally finite set $\mathcal C\subset\mathbb R^2$ has *centered disk density one* if $N_R/(\pi R^2)\to1$, where $N_R=\#(\mathcal C\cap B_R)$. A smooth function $g:(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)$ is *completely monotone* if $$(-1)^j g^{(j)}(t)\ge0\qquad(j=0,1,2,\ldots,\ t>0).$$ For such a function, define its lower energy per particle by $$E_g(\mathcal C)=\liminf_{R\to\infty}\frac1{N_R}
 \sum_{\substack{x,y\in\mathcal C\cap B_R\\x\ne y}}g(|x-y|^2).$$ The sum counts ordered pairs. Density one makes $N_R>0$ for all sufficiently large $R$. Each finite-disk sum is finite, although the lower energy and the lattice series below may be infinite.

<a id="stmt-thm-universal"></a>
<div id="thm:universal" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Universal energy minimum). *For every smooth nonnegative completely monotone function $g$ on $(0,\infty)$ and every locally finite planar set $\mathcal C$ of centered disk density one, $$E_g(\mathcal C)\ge
 \sum_{a\in A\setminus\{0\}}g(|a|^2)=E_g(A).$$ The comparison and the equality are in the extended nonnegative reals.*

</div>

For example, the theorem applies to $g(t)=t^{-p}$ for every $p>0$, including the parameters for which the lattice series diverges. A singularity of $g$ at zero causes no ambiguity in a finite sum, because only distinct points occur. The conclusion identifies the minimum; it does not classify the minimizing configurations.

Our Fourier convention is $$\widehat f(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb R^2}f(x)e^{-2\pi i x\cdot\xi}\,dx,$$ and the dual lattice is $A^*=\{\xi\in\mathbb R^2:\xi\cdot a\in\mathbb Z\text{ for every }a\in A\}$. Write $k_\alpha(x)=e^{-\pi\alpha|x|^2}$ for $\alpha>0$. The analytic statement that supplies the energy comparison concerns each Gaussian separately.

<a id="stmt-thm-gaussian"></a>
<div id="thm:gaussian" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2** (Sharp Gaussian minorants). *For every $\alpha>0$ there is a real radial Schwartz function $f_\alpha:\mathbb R^2\to\mathbb R$ such that $$f_\alpha(x)\le k_\alpha(x),\qquad
 \widehat f_\alpha(\xi)\ge0
 \quad(x,\xi\in\mathbb R^2),$$ and $$f_\alpha(a)=k_\alpha(a)\quad(a\in A\setminus\{0\}),\qquad
 \widehat f_\alpha(w)=0\quad(w\in A^*\setminus\{0\}).$$ For $\alpha\ge1$, the construction uses two finite $20$-by-$20$ interpolation blocks and an absolutely summable infinite correction.*

</div>

The two theorems have different content. The functions $f_\alpha$ give sharp analytic certificates for individual Gaussians. Positive mixtures of the resulting *energy inequalities*, rather than mixtures of the auxiliary functions, give Theorem <a href="#thm:universal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:universal">1</a>. Taking the $\alpha\ge1$ construction as input, Section <a href="#energy:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:section">2</a> proves the reciprocal-parameter extension and the complete energy deduction. The later construction and sign sections establish that input within this paper, thereby completing both theorems.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Historical context

Classical work on the lattice-restricted problem proceeds through the binary Epstein zeta function. Rankin and Cassels studied its minimization, with corrections and refinements by Ennola and Diananda (Rankin 1953; Cassels 1959, 1963; Ennola 1964; Diananda 1964) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Rankin1953](#ref-Rankin1953), [Cassels1959](#ref-Cassels1959), [Cassels1963Corrigendum](#ref-Cassels1963Corrigendum), [Ennola1964Lemma](#ref-Ennola1964Lemma), [Diananda1964](#ref-Diananda1964)]. This line of work establishes the triangular minimum for convergent inverse-power lattice sums. Montgomery’s theta theorem gives the Gaussian minimum among planar lattices of fixed covolume for every positive parameter (Montgomery 1988, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Montgomery1988](#ref-Montgomery1988)]. These lattice results do not by themselves compare the triangular lattice with a nonperiodic set.

The Fourier bounds of Cohn and Elkies for sphere packing (Cohn and Elkies 2003) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[CohnElkies2003](#ref-CohnElkies2003)] provided a framework in which pointwise inequalities for a function and its transform control a geometric optimization problem. Cohn and Kumar developed the corresponding energy bounds and formulated the Euclidean universal-optimality program (Cohn and Kumar 2007, Proposition 9.3 and Conjecture 9.4) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[CohnKumar2007](#ref-CohnKumar2007)]. In dimensions eight and twenty-four, Cohn, Kumar, Miller, Radchenko, and Viazovska proved the $E_8$ and Leech lattice cases using sharp Gaussian auxiliaries and simultaneous Fourier interpolation formulas (Cohn et al. 2022, Theorems 1.4, 1.7, and 1.9) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[CKMRV2022](#ref-CKMRV2022)].

Other planar comparisons retain restrictions on the competitors or the allowed perturbations. Faulhuber, Shafkulovska, and Zlotnikov compare the triangular lattice with the honeycomb and further periodic classes (Faulhuber, Shafkulovska, and Zlotnikov 2024, Theorems 1.1–1.2 and Corollary 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[FaulhuberShafkulovskaZlotnikov2023](#ref-FaulhuberShafkulovskaZlotnikov2023)]. Hardin and Tenpas treat four-point and six-point configurations on a fixed triangular torus and a fixed rectangular torus, respectively, with the extension from periodized Gaussians to completely monotone interactions subject to their rapid-decay hypothesis (Hardin and Tenpas 2025, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[HardinTenpas2025](#ref-HardinTenpas2025)]. For interactions of finite triangular-lattice energy, Leblé proves local optimality under sufficiently small bounded displacements, including nonperiodic displacements, with the allowed size depending on the interaction (Leblé 2025, Theorems 1–2 and Remark 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Leble2025](#ref-Leble2025)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:universal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:universal">1</a> imposes no assumption that the competitor is a bounded perturbation of a lattice.

A separate companion develops a modulo-$12$ interpolation construction for the same energy endpoint (OpenAI 2026b) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[UniversalTriangular2026](#ref-UniversalTriangular2026)]. Its finite part uses $168$ positive-node coordinates in each block, frequency quadrature, and Gaussian-parameter intervals (OpenAI 2026b, sec. 3, especially Proposition 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[UniversalTriangular2026](#ref-UniversalTriangular2026)]. The construction here uses modulo-$36$ nodes and twenty positive-node coordinates in each block. The undamped finite columns are trigonometric polynomials in squared radius, so their spectral measures are finite sums of atoms, and one containing polytope controls the Gaussian data. The auxiliary statements of the two methods are not identical, even though their energy consequences agree. The smaller finite system does not enlarge the competitor class, and it does not eliminate the infinite correction or the estimates between nodes.

The interpolation mechanism has several precedents. Cardinal formulas built from squared sine or cosine factors and value-and-derivative data occur in Cohn and Kumar’s one-dimensional energy bound (Cohn and Kumar 2007, Proposition 9.6) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[CohnKumar2007](#ref-CohnKumar2007)] and in the Gaussian extremal approximations of Carneiro, Littmann, and Vaaler (Carneiro, Littmann, and Vaaler 2013, formulas (1.2)–(1.3)) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[CarneiroLittmannVaaler2013](#ref-CarneiroLittmannVaaler2013)]. Radchenko and Viazovska developed simultaneous interpolation of an even real Schwartz function and its Fourier transform on the line (Radchenko and Viazovska 2019, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[RadchenkoViazovska2019](#ref-RadchenkoViazovska2019)]. Sardari’s planar construction uses periodic supersets of triangular quadratic-form values and shows that the values and first radial derivatives of a function and its Fourier transform at the triangular shells do not determine the function uniquely in the full radial Schwartz class (Talebizadeh Sardari 2021, sec. 6.1 and Theorem 1.7) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Sardari2021](#ref-Sardari2021)]. The uniqueness established below is only for the specified summable coefficient problem. The sharp packing companion develops Gaussian cardinal functions, Fourier kernels, finite-block and infinite-tail inversion, and Bernstein sign certification that we adapt here (OpenAI 2026a, secs. 2–4) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[SharpPlanar2026](#ref-SharpPlanar2026)]. All parameter-dependent identities and estimates required for the present Gaussian functions are proved in this paper.

For the transfer to general configurations, Cohn and de Courcy-Ireland broaden the competitor class beyond periodic configurations to the present density-based class, using a real continuous, positive-definite, integrable auxiliary satisfying their minorant condition (Cohn and Courcy-Ireland 2018, Proposition 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[CohnDeCourcyIreland2018](#ref-CohnDeCourcyIreland2018)]; see also (Cohn et al. 2022, Proposition 1.6) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[CKMRV2022](#ref-CKMRV2022)]. We prove the Schwartz case needed here locally, using a Fourier cutoff near zero. The reciprocal-parameter construction is the Gaussian instance of the unnumbered duality calculation immediately following Conjecture 6.1 in (Cohn and Miller 2016, sec. 6) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[CohnMiller2016](#ref-CohnMiller2016)]. We also prove locally the Bernstein–Widder positive Laplace representation used to mix the Gaussian kernels (Bernstein 1929; Widder 1931) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Bernstein1929](#ref-Bernstein1929), [Widder1931](#ref-Widder1931)]. Cohn–Kumar Conjecture 9.4 asks in addition for an auxiliary satisfying their Proposition 9.3 and proving sharpness for each sufficiently decaying completely monotone potential; it does not require that auxiliary to be Schwartz. Mixing the Gaussian energy inequalities does not by itself construct such an auxiliary for a general potential; the results here are the universal energy comparison and the sharp Gaussian functions.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof overview

Section <a href="#energy:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:section">2</a> first explains why the Gaussian functions have exactly the properties needed for energy. For $\mathcal C_R=\mathcal C\cap B_R$, define $M_R(\xi)=\sum_{x\in\mathcal C_R}e^{2\pi i x\cdot\xi}$. Fourier inversion gives the finite identity $$\sum_{x,y\in\mathcal C_R}f_\alpha(x-y)
 =\int_{\mathbb R^2}\widehat f_\alpha(\xi)|M_R(\xi)|^2\,d\xi.$$ A smooth cutoff of the disk and the density assumption show that, for every fixed $\varepsilon>0$, $$\liminf_{R\to\infty}\frac1{N_R}
 \int_{|\xi|\le\varepsilon}|M_R(\xi)|^2\,d\xi\ge1.$$ Nonnegativity and continuity of $\widehat f_\alpha$ therefore give the off-diagonal lower bound $\widehat f_\alpha(0)-f_\alpha(0)$, after removing the $N_R$ diagonal terms. This step uses no count in a translated smaller disk.

The contact conditions identify that lower bound. The dual lattice is a quarter turn of $A$, and Poisson summation with unit covolume gives $$\widehat f_\alpha(0)-f_\alpha(0)
 =\sum_{a\in A\setminus\{0\}}k_\alpha(a).$$ The same duality turns a minorant for $k_{1/\alpha}$ into one for $k_\alpha$ when $0<\alpha<1$, so only the construction for $\alpha\ge1$ remains. Finally a positive Laplace representation expresses $g$ as a mixture of Gaussian kernels. Fatou’s lemma is applied to their nonnegative energy sums along an arbitrary sequence of radii; this permits both an atom at the constant kernel and infinite total representing mass. These arguments prove the energy theorem from the later local construction, without requiring a sharp auxiliary for the mixed potential.

For $\alpha\ge1$, Section <a href="#sec:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:construction">3</a> works in the scalar coordinate $s=b|x|^2$. The interpolation nodes are the positive integers whose residues modulo $36$ are represented by $m^2+mn+n^2$ for integers $m,n$; they contain every coordinate $b|a|^2$ for $a\in A\setminus\{0\}$ and may include additional nodes. A product of squared sine factors, one for each residue, has a double zero at every node. Multiplying it by suitable double- and simple-pole factors produces columns that prescribe a value and a derivative. Periodic pole columns handle ten positive nodes, together with fixed data at zero; rational columns with one pole location handle the remaining positive nodes.

The periodic columns use stronger Gaussian damping. Before damping they are trigonometric polynomials, so their spectral measures consist of finitely many atoms. They also contribute value-and-derivative data at later nodes in their residue classes modulo $36$; the stronger damping makes these repeated contributions summably small. The rational columns have absolutely continuous spectral measures with total variation bounded uniformly in their node. These representations prove that arbitrary absolutely summable tail coefficients give radial Schwartz functions, with all needed differentiations justified. Two real input functions $U_1,U_2$ are formed from the damped columns, together with opposite fixed multiples of the damped sine product. Define $$F_1=U_1+\widehat U_2,\qquad F_2=U_2+\widehat U_1.$$ Fourier inversion and radiality give $\widehat F_1=F_2$.

Section <a href="#sec:finite-certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite-certificate">4</a> constructs a finite approximation to the simultaneous value-and-slope equations. There are twenty unknown coordinates on each side; taking sums and differences separates the forty coupled coordinates into two blocks of order twenty. A single containing polytope encloses the Gaussian data. Outward-rational interval arithmetic certifies the fixed inverse bounds and coefficient bounds on that polytope. For the polynomial inequalities, every Bernstein coefficient is bounded on the whole polytope, with no mesh of parameter or spatial values. Appendix <a href="#app:finite-certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:finite-certificate">7</a> gives the exact evaluation recipes and proves that their interval arithmetic encloses the required quantities.

Section <a href="#sec:infinite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:infinite">5</a> then proves uniform bounds for the maps between the finite lists and the full unweighted $\ell^1$ tail space. These analytic bounds include the repeated jets of the periodic columns. Together with the finite inverse bounds, they permit a Schur elimination and a Neumann series on the full tail space. The resulting functions satisfy the interpolation equations *exactly* at every node, including those beyond the finite block. The same argument bounds their difference from the finite approximation. Thus finite arithmetic supplies quantitative inputs, while the full-space argument supplies the exact infinite interpolation.

Section <a href="#sec:global-signs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:global-signs">6</a> proves the remaining pointwise inequalities on the entire half-line. Near a node, subtract the exact value and linear term before dividing by the squared displacement. The finite polynomial tests, correction bounds, and Gaussian comparison then control the exact functions on whole half-cells; a separate cubic argument handles the first right half-cell. Farther out, signed sine-product terms dominate the remaining curvature on every gap. Positive normalization gives the $\alpha\ge1$ minorant and closes the input to the energy reduction.

<a id="energy:section"></a>
### Energy from Gaussian minorants

We first isolate what the Gaussian construction must accomplish. Assuming the half-range statement <a href="#energy:input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:input">[energy:input]</a> below, this section extends it to every positive Gaussian parameter and derives Theorem <a href="#thm:universal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:universal">1</a>. Sections <a href="#sec:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:construction">3</a>–<a href="#sec:global-signs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:global-signs">6</a> will then establish the assumed statement. This order separates the configuration-level argument from the interpolation certificate.

The competitor needs only a centered disk density; no estimate on the number of points in a smaller translated disk will be used. The corresponding density-only linear programming statement is (Cohn and Courcy-Ireland 2018, Definition 1.1 and Proposition 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[CohnDeCourcyIreland2018](#ref-CohnDeCourcyIreland2018)]. We give the needed Schwartz-function case by concentrating a Fourier test near zero.

<a id="energy:input"></a>
For every $\alpha\ge1$, the construction will supply a real radial Schwartz function $f_\alpha$ such that $$\begin{gathered}
 f_\alpha\le k_\alpha,\qquad \widehat f_\alpha\ge0,\\
 f_\alpha(a)=k_\alpha(a)\quad(a\in A\setminus\{0\}),\qquad
 \widehat f_\alpha(w)=0\quad(w\in A^*\setminus\{0\}).
 \end{gathered}
 \label{energy:input}$$ We first extend this statement to all positive Gaussian parameters and identify the exact lower bound it supplies.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Duality and the lattice normalization

The following lattice calculation is independent of the input <a href="#energy:input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:input">[energy:input]</a>. Let $M$ have columns $b^{-1/2}(1,0)$ and $b^{-1/2}(1/2,b)$, so that $A=M\mathbb Z^2$ and $\det M=1$. If $J=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, the two-dimensional identity $M^{-\mathsf T}=JMJ^{-1}$ gives $$A^*=M^{-\mathsf T}\mathbb Z^2=JA,$$ because $J^{-1}\mathbb Z^2=\mathbb Z^2$. Thus $A$ and $A^*$ have identical sets of radii, and the covolume in Poisson summation is exactly one.

<a id="stmt-energy-reciprocal"></a>
<div id="energy:reciprocal" class="lemma">

<a id="energy:poisson"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Reciprocal Gaussian parameters). *If <a href="#energy:input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:input">[energy:input]</a> holds for every $\alpha\ge1$, it holds for every $\alpha>0$. For each of these functions, $$\widehat f_\alpha(0)-f_\alpha(0)
 =\sum_{a\in A\setminus\{0\}}e^{-\pi\alpha|a|^2}.
 \label{energy:poisson}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-energy-reciprocal"><summary>Proof of stmt-energy-reciprocal</summary>

*Proof.* The Fourier complement follows the unnumbered duality calculation after Conjecture 6.1 in (Cohn and Miller 2016, sec. 6, p. 19) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[CohnMiller2016](#ref-CohnMiller2016)]. For $0<\alpha<1$, set $\beta=1/\alpha$ and define $$f_\alpha=k_\alpha-\alpha^{-1}\widehat f_\beta.$$ The planar Gaussian transform is $\widehat k_\alpha=\alpha^{-1}k_{1/\alpha}$; the real case of the Gaussian integral proved in <a href="#analytic:complex-gaussian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="analytic:complex-gaussian">[analytic:complex-gaussian]</a> gives this formula in our Fourier convention. Fourier inversion reflects the argument, and $f_\beta$ is even; hence $$k_\alpha-f_\alpha=\alpha^{-1}\widehat f_\beta\ge0,
 \qquad
 \widehat f_\alpha=\alpha^{-1}(k_\beta-f_\beta)\ge0.$$ Both functions remain real radial Schwartz functions. Radiality and $A^*=JA$ imply $\widehat f_\beta(a)=0$ for $a\in A\setminus\{0\}$ and $f_\beta(w)=k_\beta(w)$ for $w\in A^*\setminus\{0\}$. These are exactly the two equalities required for $f_\alpha$.

For completeness, periodize $f_\alpha$ over $A$. Schwartz decay makes $\sum_{a\in A}f_\alpha(x+a)$ converge uniformly on compact sets with every derivative. Its Fourier coefficient at $w\in A^*$, obtained by integration over a unit-area fundamental cell, is $\widehat f_\alpha(w)$. These coefficients are absolutely summable, again by Schwartz decay. Evaluation of the Fourier series at zero gives $$\sum_{a\in A}f_\alpha(a)=\sum_{w\in A^*}\widehat f_\alpha(w).$$ Substitute the two interpolation conditions in <a href="#energy:input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:input">[energy:input]</a> and isolate the terms at zero to obtain <a href="#energy:poisson" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:poisson">[energy:poisson]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### A Fourier comparison using only centered density

Let $\mathcal C\subset\mathbb R^2$ be locally finite, put $\mathcal C_R=\mathcal C\cap B_R$ and $N_R=\#\mathcal C_R$, and assume $N_R/(\pi R^2)\to1$. In particular, $N_R>0$ for all sufficiently large $R$.

<a id="stmt-energy-low-frequency"></a>
<div id="energy:low-frequency" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Fourier mass near zero). *For every fixed $\varepsilon>0$, the exponential sum $M_R(\xi)=\sum_{x\in\mathcal C_R}e^{2\pi i x\cdot\xi}$ satisfies <a id="energy:fourier-mass"></a>$$\liminf_{R\to\infty}\frac1{N_R}
 \int_{|\xi|\le\varepsilon}|M_R(\xi)|^2\,d\xi\ge1.
 \label{energy:fourier-mass}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-energy-low-frequency"><summary>Proof of stmt-energy-low-frequency</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $d>0$ and choose a smooth compactly supported function $\phi$ with $0\le\phi\le1$, equal to one on $B_1$, and supported in $B_{1+d}$. Fourier inversion gives $$\int_{\mathbb R^2}M_R(\xi)R^2\widehat\phi(R\xi)\,d\xi
 =\sum_{x\in\mathcal C_R}\phi(x/R)=N_R.$$ The integral is absolutely convergent, since the sum is finite and $\widehat\phi$ is Schwartz. The elementary bound $|M_R|\le N_R$ shows that the integral omitted on restricting to $|\xi|\le\varepsilon$ has modulus at most $$N_R\int_{|u|>\varepsilon R}|\widehat\phi(u)|\,du
 =N_R\delta_R,\qquad \delta_R\longrightarrow0.$$ Cauchy–Schwarz and Plancherel therefore give, for all sufficiently large $R$, $$\begin{aligned}
 N_R^2(1-\delta_R)^2
 &\le
 \left(\int_{|\xi|\le\varepsilon}|M_R(\xi)|^2\,d\xi\right)
 \left(\int_{\mathbb R^2}|R^2\widehat\phi(R\xi)|^2\,d\xi\right)\\
 &=R^2\|\phi\|_2^2
 \int_{|\xi|\le\varepsilon}|M_R(\xi)|^2\,d\xi.
\end{aligned}$$ Since $\|\phi\|_2^2\le\pi(1+d)^2$, division by $N_R$ and passage to the lower limit yield the lower bound $(1+d)^{-2}$. Let $d\downarrow0$. This argument uses only the number $N_R$ and the fact that every point being summed lies in $B_R$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-energy-lp"></a>
<div id="energy:lp" class="proposition">

<a id="energy:lp-bound"></a>
**Proposition 5** (Density-only linear programming). *Let $f$ be a real even Schwartz function on $\mathbb R^2$ with $\widehat f\ge0$, and let $\mathcal C$ be locally finite with $N_R/(\pi R^2)\to1$. Then $$\liminf_{R\to\infty}\frac1{N_R}
 \sum_{\substack{x,y\in\mathcal C_R\\x\ne y}}f(x-y)
 \ge\widehat f(0)-f(0).
 \label{energy:lp-bound}$$ The same lower bound holds if the summands are replaced by $\Phi(x-y)$, where $\Phi(z)\ge f(z)$ for every $z\ne0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-energy-lp"><summary>Proof of stmt-energy-lp</summary>

*Proof.* Fourier inversion and the finiteness of $\mathcal C_R$ give the exact identity $$\sum_{x,y\in\mathcal C_R}f(x-y)
 =\int_{\mathbb R^2}\widehat f(\xi)|M_R(\xi)|^2\,d\xi.$$ Write $a=\widehat f(0)\ge0$. If $a=0$, the right side is already nonnegative. If $a>0$, fix $0<\delta<a$ and choose $\varepsilon>0$ so that $\widehat f(\xi)\ge a-\delta$ whenever $|\xi|\le\varepsilon$. Nonnegativity on the complement and Lemma <a href="#energy:low-frequency" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:low-frequency">4</a> imply that the lower limit of the displayed expression divided by $N_R$ is at least $a-\delta$. Letting $\delta\downarrow0$ proves the diagonal-included bound $a$. There are exactly $N_R$ diagonal terms, each equal to $f(0)$. Their removal proves <a href="#energy:lp-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:lp-bound">[energy:lp-bound]</a>; the assertion for $\Phi$ follows by termwise comparison of finite sums. ◻

</details>

<a id="energy:gaussian"></a>
Under the input <a href="#energy:input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:input">[energy:input]</a>, Lemma <a href="#energy:reciprocal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:reciprocal">3</a> and Proposition <a href="#energy:lp" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:lp">5</a> give $$\liminf_{R\to\infty}\frac1{N_R}
 \sum_{\substack{x,y\in\mathcal C_R\\x\ne y}}e^{-\pi\alpha|x-y|^2}
 \ge \sum_{a\in A\setminus\{0\}}e^{-\pi\alpha|a|^2}
 \qquad(\alpha>0).
 \label{energy:gaussian}$$ Only nonnegative kernels will be mixed in the remaining argument; there is no need to integrate the auxiliary functions themselves.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Positive mixtures without a boundedness assumption at zero

The next lemma is the classical Bernstein representation of completely monotone functions; see (Widder 1931, Theorem 8) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Widder1931](#ref-Widder1931)]. For a related construction using Taylor coefficients and step functions, see (Dubourdieu 1940, secs. I–II) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Dubourdieu1940](#ref-Dubourdieu1940)]. We give the details here on the open half-line for a locally finite measure that may have infinite total mass and an atom at zero.

<a id="stmt-energy-laplace"></a>
<div id="energy:laplace" class="lemma">

<a id="energy:laplace-representation"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Positive Laplace representation). *Suppose $g:(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)$ is smooth and $(-1)^j g^{(j)}(s)\ge0$ for every integer $j\ge0$ and every $s>0$. There is a positive locally finite Borel measure $\nu$ on $[0,\infty)$ such that $$g(s)=\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{-st}\,d\nu(t)\qquad(s>0).
 \label{energy:laplace-representation}$$ The integral is finite for every $s>0$; the mass $\nu([0,\infty))$ need not be finite.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-energy-laplace"><summary>Proof of stmt-energy-laplace</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $l>0$ and put $q(x)=g(l-x)$ for $0\le x<l$. Every derivative of $q$ is nonnegative. Taylor’s theorem gives $$q(x)=\sum_{j=0}^m b_j(l)x^j+R_m(x),\qquad
 b_j(l)=\frac{(-1)^j g^{(j)}(l)}{j!}\ge0,$$ where $$R_m(x)=\frac1{m!}\int_0^x q^{(m+1)}(u)(x-u)^m\,du\ge0.$$ For $0<x<y<l$ and $0\le u\le x$, $(x-u)/(y-u)\le x/y$. Comparing the nonnegative remainder integrals therefore yields $$0\le R_m(x)\le(x/y)^mR_m(y)\le(x/y)^m g(l-y).$$ The right side tends to zero. Thus $g(l-x)=\sum_{j\ge0}b_j(l)x^j$ for every $0\le x<l$.

<a id="energy:approximate-transform"></a>
For positive integers $l$, define the locally finite positive measure $$\nu_l=\sum_{j\ge0}b_j(l)l^j\delta_{j/l}.$$ The identity just proved, evaluated at $x=le^{-s/l}<l$, gives $$L_l(s):=\int e^{-st}\,d\nu_l(t)
 =g\bigl(l(1-e^{-s/l})\bigr)\longrightarrow g(s)
 \qquad(s>0).
 \label{energy:approximate-transform}$$ For each $T>0$, $\nu_l([0,T])\le e^T L_l(1)$ is uniformly bounded in $l$. We record explicitly the elementary measure compactness being used. Extend the cumulative functions $H_l(t)=\nu_l([0,t])$ by zero to $t<0$. A diagonal subsequence makes $H_l(q)$ converge to $a(q)$ for every rational $q$; the values at each fixed rational are bounded. The right-continuous nondecreasing function $$H(t)=\inf_{\substack{q>t\\q\in\mathbb Q}}a(q)$$ is zero for $t<0$ and defines a locally finite Stieltjes measure $\nu$ supported on $[0,\infty)$. Rational bracketing gives $H_l(t)\to H(t)$ at every continuity point of $H$. Step approximation on compact intervals, choosing their endpoints at continuity points, then gives $$\int\psi\,d\nu_l\longrightarrow\int\psi\,d\nu
 \qquad\bigl(\psi\in C_c([0,\infty))\bigr)$$ along this one subsequence. For intervals meeting zero the same argument starts at a negative endpoint; it therefore retains any atom at zero.

It remains to pass from compactly supported tests to Laplace transforms. Fix $s>0$ and put $C_s=\sup_l L_l(s/2)<\infty$. Compactly supported nonnegative cutoffs and the preceding convergence imply $\int e^{-st/2}\,d\nu(t)\le C_s$. For either $\mu=\nu_l$ or $\mu=\nu$ we consequently have $$\int_{t>T}e^{-st}\,d\mu(t)\le C_s e^{-sT/2}.$$ Apply compact-test convergence to a continuous cutoff of $e^{-st}$ that equals it on $[0,T]$, then let $T\to\infty$. It follows that $L_l(s)\to\int e^{-st}\,d\nu(t)$ along the chosen subsequence. Combining this with <a href="#energy:approximate-transform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:approximate-transform">[energy:approximate-transform]</a> proves <a href="#energy:laplace-representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:laplace-representation">[energy:laplace-representation]</a> for every $s>0$. The subsequence was chosen independently of $s$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Lower limits, singular interactions, and attainment

We now prove the energy theorem under the single input <a href="#energy:input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:input">[energy:input]</a>. Once Sections <a href="#sec:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:construction">3</a>–<a href="#sec:global-signs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:global-signs">6</a> establish that input, Lemma <a href="#energy:reciprocal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:reciprocal">3</a> also completes the all-parameter Gaussian theorem.

<details id="proof-stmt-energy-laplace-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-energy-laplace</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:universal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:universal">1</a> from <a href="#energy:input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:input">[energy:input]</a>.* Let $\nu$ be supplied by Lemma <a href="#energy:laplace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:laplace">6</a>. Choose any sequence $R_n\to\infty$, discarding finitely many terms so that $N_{R_n}>0$, and put $$A_n(t)=\frac1{N_{R_n}}
 \sum_{\substack{x,y\in\mathcal C_{R_n}\\x\ne y}}
 e^{-t|x-y|^2},\qquad
 L(t)=\sum_{a\in A\setminus\{0\}}e^{-t|a|^2}.$$ These are nonnegative Borel functions of $t\ge0$. For every $t>0$, <a href="#energy:gaussian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:gaussian">[energy:gaussian]</a> with $\alpha=t/\pi$ gives $\liminf_n A_n(t)\ge L(t)$. This also holds at $t=0$, because $A_n(0)=N_{R_n}-1\to\infty=L(0)$. The identity for $g$, followed by Fatou’s lemma and Tonelli’s theorem, now gives $$\begin{aligned}
 &\liminf_n\frac1{N_{R_n}}
 \sum_{\substack{x,y\in\mathcal C_{R_n}\\x\ne y}}g(|x-y|^2)\\
 &\qquad=\liminf_n\int A_n(t)\,d\nu(t)
 \ge\int\liminf_n A_n(t)\,d\nu(t)
 \ge\int L(t)\,d\nu(t)
 =\sum_{a\in A\setminus\{0\}}g(|a|^2).
\end{aligned}$$ Every step is valid in the extended nonnegative reals. Local finiteness makes each disk sum finite, and its distinct-point distances are positive, so the possible singularity of $g$ at zero does not enter a finite sum. No common minimizing sequence in the Gaussian parameter was selected: its lower bound holds along every sequence of radii. Since the sequence $R_n$ was arbitrary, the full radius lower-limit inequality follows.

Finally, choose a bounded fundamental cell of $A$ with zero in its closure; its area is one. Comparing its translates with disks whose radii differ by its diameter proves $\#(A\cap B_R)/(\pi R^2)\to1$. For any fixed $v\in A\setminus\{0\}$, the number $N_R(v)$ of $x\in A\cap B_R$ for which $x+v\in B_R$ satisfies $$\#(A\cap B_{R-|v|})\le N_R(v)\le\#(A\cap B_R),$$ for $R>|v|$. Hence $N_R(v)/\#(A\cap B_R)\to1$. For any finite set $D\subset A\setminus\{0\}$, retaining the ordered displacements in $D$ gives a lower limit at least $\sum_{v\in D}g(|v|^2)$. Taking the supremum over $D$ gives the full lattice sum, even when it diverges. Conversely, for each $x\in A\cap B_R$, its finite neighbor sum is at most the full nonnegative lattice sum. When that sum is finite, averaging gives the matching upper bound. These observations prove the assertion about the lattice limit. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:construction"></a>
### The Gaussian interpolation construction

It remains to establish <a href="#energy:input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:input">[energy:input]</a> for $\alpha\ge1$. We construct a Fourier pair by prescribing values and derivatives at a periodic set of squared radii. The finite part uses trigonometric columns; stronger Gaussian damping makes their value-and-derivative contributions at later nodes in their residue classes modulo $36$ summably small. The infinite part uses columns with one removable pole each. Their spectral formulas will supply bounds uniform in the column index, which is the input needed to solve all interpolation equations at once.

Keep the Fourier convention, $b=\sqrt3/2$, and the lattice $A$ from the introduction. Set $$B=b^{-2}=\frac43,\quad
 h=\frac{17}{50},\quad H=\frac{27}{50},\quad
 \eta=H-h=\frac15,\qquad s=b|x|^2.$$ The same coordinate $s=b|\xi|^2$ is used for frequency-space radial functions. A prime denotes differentiation in this scalar coordinate. The covolume-one and quarter-turn duality calculation in Section <a href="#energy:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:section">2</a> shows that $A$ and $A^*$ have the same radii. For the displayed basis of $A$, the squared-radius coordinates are precisely the nonnegative values of $m^2+mn+n^2$.

Define $$\begin{aligned}
 I&=\{0,1,3,4,7,9,12,13,16,19,21,25,27,28,31\},\\
 \mathcal N&=(36\mathbb Z+I)\cap(0,\infty),\\
 f&=\{1,3,4,7,9,12,13,16,19,21\},\qquad
 T=\mathcal N\setminus f,\qquad L=\{0\}\cup f.
\end{aligned}$$ Every nonzero lattice squared-radius coordinate belongs to $\mathcal N$. Indeed, modulo $3$ the quadratic form equals $(m-n)^2$. If $m-n$ is not divisible by $3$, its residue modulo $9$ is one of $1,4,7$; if $m=n+3j$, the form is $3n^2$ modulo $9$, hence is $0$ or $3$. Modulo $4$ its possible values, checked by the parities and residues of $m,n$, are $0,1,3$. Combining these two lists by the Chinese remainder theorem gives exactly the displayed residue set $I$. We impose interpolation at this superset, without needing it to consist entirely of actual lattice shells.

<a id="analytic:local-product"></a>
<a id="analytic:input-functions"></a>
Set $$\kappa=\pi/36,\qquad t_j=j/18,\qquad
 P(s)=\prod_{a\in I}(2\sin\kappa(s-a))^2
      =\sum_{j=-15}^{15}P_j e^{i\pi t_js}.$$ The zeros are precisely $36\mathbb Z+I$, each of order two. For a zero $n$ define $Q_n>0$ and $D_n\in\mathbb R$ by $$P(n+u)=Q_nu^2(1+D_nu+O(u^2)).
 \label{analytic:local-product}$$ Both $Q_n$ and $D_n$ are periodic with period $36$. The rational tail columns and their Gaussian spectral treatment below adapt (OpenAI 2026a, Lemma 2.1 and Proposition 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[SharpPlanar2026](#ref-SharpPlanar2026)] and (OpenAI 2026b, Lemmas 2.2 and 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[UniversalTriangular2026](#ref-UniversalTriangular2026)]. The paired double and simple poles also occur in the one-dimensional constructions of (Cohn and Kumar 2007, Proposition 9.6) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[CohnKumar2007](#ref-CohnKumar2007)] and (Carneiro, Littmann, and Vaaler 2013, formulas (1.2)–(1.3)) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[CarneiroLittmannVaaler2013](#ref-CarneiroLittmannVaaler2013)]. For each side $i=1,2$ introduce an input radial function $U_i$ by $$\begin{aligned}
 U_i(s)={}&e^{-\pi Hs}P(s)\sum_{n\in L}
 \left(c_{i,n}\kappa^2\csc^2\kappa(s-n)
                 +d_{i,n}\kappa\cot\kappa(s-n)\right)
       +C_i e^{-\pi hs}P(s)\notag\\
 &+e^{-\pi hs}P(s)\sum_{n\in T}
 \left(\frac{c_{i,n}}{(s-n)^2}+\frac{d_{i,n}}{s-n}\right).
 \label{analytic:input-functions}
\end{aligned}$$ Every apparent pole is removed by continuity. The tail coefficient lists will lie in $\ell^1(T;\mathbb R^2)$ with norm $\sum_{n\in T}(|c_{i,n}|+|d_{i,n}|)$. Fix $$c_{1,0}=c_{2,0}=\frac{11}{25},\quad
 d_{1,0}=d_{2,0}=0,\qquad
 C_1=-\frac3{500},\quad C_2=\frac3{500}.$$ The notation $U_i(s)$ denotes the same function as $x\mapsto U_i(b|x|^2)$; Fourier transforms always refer to the latter function on $\mathbb R^2$. Define $$F_1=U_1+\widehat U_2,\qquad F_2=U_2+\widehat U_1,
 \qquad J_i(s)=e^{\pi hs}F_i(s).$$ The spectral argument proves that $U_i$ and $F_i$ are real radial Schwartz functions and that $F_2=\widehat F_1$ for every summable coefficient list. The divided functions $J_i$ need not be Schwartz.

For $\alpha\ge1$, put $$z=\frac1{\pi(\alpha/b-h)}>0,
 \qquad G_z(s)=Q_1z e^{(1-s)/z}.$$ Our interpolation equations are <a id="analytic:all-positive-jets"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 J_1(n)&=G_z(n),& J_1'(n)&=G_z'(n),\notag\\
 J_2(n)&=0,& J_2'(n)&=0
       &&(n\in\mathcal N).
 \label{analytic:all-positive-jets}
\end{aligned}$$ The sign argument will additionally establish $J_1(s)\le G_z(s)$ and $J_2(s)\ge0$ for $s\ge0$. Then $F_1/(Q_1ze^{1/z})$ is the required Gaussian minorant, because $$e^{-\pi hs}G_z(s)/(Q_1ze^{1/z})
 =e^{-(\pi h+1/z)s}=e^{-\pi\alpha|x|^2}.$$ The infinite system below establishes all the jets exactly, before any estimates between nodes are invoked.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Spectral measures for the interpolation columns

We establish exact spectral formulas for the columns and prove that summable coefficient lists define Schwartz functions. These facts justify the infinite interpolation system and termwise differentiation.

A finite complex measure $\mu$ on $[-5/6,5/6]$ will be called conjugate symmetric if reflection takes it to its complex conjugate. Then $\int e^{i\pi ts}\,d\mu(t)$ is real for real $s$. Its folded measure is $$\mu_+=\mu(\{0\})\delta_0+2\mu|_{(0,5/6]},
 \qquad
 \int e^{i\pi ts}\,d\mu(t)
 =\operatorname{Re}\int_{[0,5/6]}e^{i\pi ts}\,d\mu_+(t).$$ The atom at zero is not doubled. The total variations of these two measures agree, because the atom at zero is real and the positive and negative parts have equal total variation.

<a id="analytic:atomic-c"></a>
<a id="analytic:atomic-d"></a>
For $n\in L$ consider the entire, real-valued columns obtained by removing their apparent poles: $$A_n(s)=\kappa^2 P(s)\csc^2\kappa(s-n),\qquad
 B_n(s)=\kappa P(s)\cot\kappa(s-n).$$ Set $b_j=2-\mathbf1_{j=0}$. Their folded spectral measures are atomic at $t_j$, $0\le j\le15$, with respective masses $$\begin{aligned}
 M_{j;c,n}
 &=-4\kappa^2b_j
     \sum_{l=j+1}^{15}(l-j)P_l e^{i\pi(t_l-t_j)n},
 \label{analytic:atomic-c}\\
 M_{j;d,n}
 &=i\kappa b_j\left(P_j+
     2\sum_{l=j+1}^{15}P_l e^{i\pi(t_l-t_j)n}\right).
 \label{analytic:atomic-d}
\end{aligned}$$ The folded masses for the column $P(s)$ itself are $V_j=b_jP_j$.

To prove these identities, put $u=s-n$, $Y=e^{2i\kappa u}$, and $a_l=P_l e^{i\pi t_ln}$. The Laurent polynomial $P(n+u)=\sum_l a_lY^l$ has a factor $(Y-1)^2$. Moreover, $$\csc^2\kappa u=-\frac{4Y}{(Y-1)^2},\qquad
 \cot\kappa u=i\left(1+\frac2{Y-1}\right).$$ The products are therefore Laurent polynomials. Expanding at infinity, $$\frac{Y}{(Y-1)^2}=\sum_{r\ge1}rY^{-r},\qquad
 \frac1{Y-1}=\sum_{r\ge1}Y^{-r},$$ shows that the coefficients of $Y^j$, $j\ge0$, in these products are $-4\kappa^2\sum_{l>j}(l-j)a_l$ and $i\kappa(a_j+2\sum_{l>j}a_l)$. Changing back from $Y^j$ to $e^{i\pi t_js}$ gives <a href="#analytic:atomic-c" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="analytic:atomic-c">[analytic:atomic-c]</a>–<a href="#analytic:atomic-d" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="analytic:atomic-d">[analytic:atomic-d]</a> after folding. The negative coefficients are conjugates of the positive coefficients. For completeness, $P(n)=P'(n)=0$ give $$P_0+2\operatorname{Re}\sum_{l>0}a_l=0,
 \qquad \operatorname{Im}\sum_{l>0}l a_l=0.$$ Thus both displayed zero-frequency masses are real, as folding requires.

<a id="analytic:tail-c"></a>
<a id="analytic:tail-d"></a>
For $n\in T$ the other two undamped columns are $$C_n(s)=\frac{P(s)}{(s-n)^2},\qquad
 D_n^{\mathrm{col}}(s)=\frac{P(s)}{s-n},$$ again with removable values. Their folded spectral measures are absolutely continuous. On $t\in(t_{j-1},t_j)$, $1\le j\le15$, their densities are $$\begin{aligned}
 \rho_{c,n}(t)
 &=-2\pi^2\sum_{l=j}^{15}(t_l-t)P_l
                     e^{i\pi(t_l-t)n},\label{analytic:tail-c}\\
 \rho_{d,n}(t)
 &=2i\pi\sum_{l=j}^{15}P_l
                     e^{i\pi(t_l-t)n}.\label{analytic:tail-d}
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed, for real $a$ the oriented integral identities $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{e^{i\pi au}-1}{u}
 &=i\pi\int_0^a e^{i\pi tu}\,dt,\\
 \frac{e^{i\pi au}-1-i\pi au}{u^2}
 &=-\pi^2\int_0^a(a-t)e^{i\pi tu}\,dt
\end{aligned}$$ hold first for $u\ne0$ and then by continuity for $u=0$. They are valid also when $a<0$, with the usual orientation of the integral. Since $\sum_l a_l=\sum_l t_la_l=0$, summing these identities with $a=t_l$ and coefficient $a_l$ gives the asserted columns. Replacing $u$ by $s-n$ yields the phase in <a href="#analytic:tail-c" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="analytic:tail-c">[analytic:tail-c]</a>–<a href="#analytic:tail-d" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="analytic:tail-d">[analytic:tail-d]</a>. The negative-frequency densities are the conjugates of their reflected positive-frequency densities. The measures are absolutely continuous even at zero; endpoint values of their densities are immaterial. In particular, no zero-frequency atom is to be inserted when folding.

<a id="analytic:tail-variation"></a>
The phases depending on $n$ have modulus one. Consequently the variation of either folded measure on $[t_{j-1},t_j]$ is bounded by the corresponding entry of $$\label{analytic:tail-variation}
 \begin{gathered}
 W_{j,*}=\left(
  2(\pi/18)^2\sum_{l=j}^{15}(l-j+\tfrac12)|P_l|,
  (2\pi/18)\sum_{l=j}^{15}|P_l|\right),
 \\ W_{0,*}=(0,0).
 \end{gathered}$$ For the first entry, integrate $t_l-t$ on this interval to obtain $(l-j+1/2)/18^2$; for the second, the interval length is $1/18$. These bounds hold uniformly for every positive tail node $n$, however large it is.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Schwartz convergence and the Fourier waves

For $k>0$ and $|t|\le5/6$ put $$\Phi_{k,t}(x)=e^{-\pi b(k-it)|x|^2},\qquad x\in\mathbb R^2.$$ For each pair of multi-indices $\beta,\gamma$ there is a finite constant $C_{\beta,\gamma,k}$, independent of $t$, such that <a id="analytic:schwartz-uniform"></a>$$\label{analytic:schwartz-uniform}
 \sup_{x\in\mathbb R^2}
  |x^\beta\partial_x^\gamma\Phi_{k,t}(x)|
 \le C_{\beta,\gamma,k}.$$ To see this directly, differentiating the Gaussian gives a polynomial in $x$ with coefficients polynomial in $b(k-it)$, times the same Gaussian. Those coefficients are uniformly bounded on the compact frequency interval, and the absolute value of the Gaussian is $e^{-\pi bk|x|^2}$. Every polynomial times this fixed decaying Gaussian is bounded.

Thus integration of $\Phi_{k,t}$ against a finite measure defines a Schwartz function, and its $(\beta,\gamma)$ seminorm is at most $C_{\beta,\gamma,k}$ times the measure’s total variation. Differentiation under the integral follows from the same bounds. In particular, define $$w_c=\sum_{j=1}^{15}W_{j,c},\qquad
 w_d=\sum_{j=1}^{15}W_{j,d}.$$ For any real lists satisfying $\sum_{n\in T}(|c_n|+|d_n|)<\infty$, the series of tail measures converges in total variation and has total variation at most $$w_c\sum_{n\in T}|c_n|+w_d\sum_{n\in T}|d_n|.$$ The corresponding series of functions therefore converges in every Schwartz seminorm. This proves the claimed convergence for arbitrary summable coefficients; no moment condition such as $\sum n^r|c_n|<\infty$ is needed. It also justifies taking removable values and derivatives at every node. Equivalently, before damping, the $r$th $s$-derivative of each integral is bounded on the real axis by $(5\pi/6)^r$ times its total variation, uniformly in $n$. The finitely many atomic columns cause no further convergence issue.

<a id="analytic:complex-gaussian"></a>
With Fourier kernel $e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\xi}$ the Gaussian integral in dimension two is $$\label{analytic:complex-gaussian}
 \widehat\Phi_{k,t}(\xi)
 =\frac1{b(k-it)}
   \exp\left(-\frac{\pi|\xi|^2}{b(k-it)}\right).$$ For a positive real parameter $a$, the one-dimensional identity $\widehat{e^{-\pi ax^2}}(\xi)=a^{-1/2}e^{-\pi\xi^2/a}$ follows by differentiating the Fourier integral in $\xi$, integrating by parts, and using $\int e^{-\pi ax^2}\,dx=a^{-1/2}$ at $\xi=0$. Taking the product of two such integrals gives the two-dimensional formula. Both sides of that formula are holomorphic in $a$ on $\operatorname{Re}a>0$: compact subsets have a common integrable Gaussian bound for every parameter derivative. The identity theorem extends the formula to that half-plane and gives <a href="#analytic:complex-gaussian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="analytic:complex-gaussian">[analytic:complex-gaussian]</a> with $a=b(k-it)$. This argument fixes both the Fourier normalization and the complex prefactor without any square-root branch ambiguity in dimension two.

<a id="analytic:fourier-wave"></a>
Write $s=b|\xi|^2$, $B=b^{-2}$, and set $$\label{analytic:fourier-wave}
 X_k(t)=\frac{B}{k^2+t^2},\qquad
 \lambda_k(t)=\frac{k+it}{b(k^2+t^2)},\qquad
 \zeta_{k,v}(t)=-tX_k(t)+i\bigl(kX_k(t)-v\bigr).$$ After division by $e^{-\pi vs}$, the right side of <a href="#analytic:complex-gaussian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="analytic:complex-gaussian">[analytic:complex-gaussian]</a> is exactly $\lambda_k(t)e^{i\pi\zeta_{k,v}(t)s}$. The Fourier transform commutes with the above measure integrals by Fubini, since $\int|\Phi_{k,t}(x)|\,dx=1/(bk)$ uniformly in $t$. It also commutes with summation of the tail columns, either by the same domination or by convergence in the Schwartz space. At opposite frequencies the transformed waves are conjugates, so the same folded measures may be used, followed by taking the real part.

For $k,v\in\{h,H\}$ the additional decay exponent is positive. Indeed $kX_k(t)$ decreases as $t^2$ increases; its smallest difference from $v$ is attained at $|t|=5/6$. With $h=17/50$ and $H=27/50$, the four endpoint differences, for $(k,v)=(h,h),(h,H),(H,h),(H,H)$, are respectively $$\frac{100079}{455650},\quad
 \frac{8949}{455650},\quad
 \frac{216419}{554650},\quad
 \frac{105489}{554650}.$$ In particular each is positive. The undivided transformed waves also have uniform Schwartz bounds: their Gaussian parameters $1/(b(k-it))$ form a compact subset of the open right half-plane. All input functions and their transforms are consequently real radial Schwartz functions. If $U_1,U_2$ denote the two complete input sums, then $$F_1=U_1+\widehat U_2,\qquad F_2=U_2+\widehat U_1
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \widehat F_1=F_2,$$ because Fourier inversion gives $\widehat{\widehat U_2}(x)=U_2(-x)$ and $U_2$ is radial.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Jets and repeated nodes

<a id="analytic:direct-jets"></a>
At a positive node $n$ let $v=H$ on $f$ and $v=h$ on $T$. After division of the input centered at $n$ by $e^{-\pi vs}$, its value and first derivative at $n$ are $$\label{analytic:direct-jets}
 Q_n\bigl(c_n,d_n+D_nc_n\bigr).$$ For the rational columns this follows immediately from <a href="#analytic:local-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="analytic:local-product">[analytic:local-product]</a>. For the trigonometric columns use $$\kappa^2\csc^2\kappa u=u^{-2}+O(1),\qquad
 \kappa\cot\kappa u=u^{-1}+O(u).$$ Every other direct column has zero value and first derivative at $n$, except for a trigonometric column with the same residue modulo $36$. The direct constant column $P$ always has a double zero there.

<a id="analytic:transformed-jet"></a>
For any additional divided contribution $Z(s)$, cancelling its jets requires the coefficient correction $$Q_n^{-1}\bigl(-Z(n),D_nZ(n)-Z'(n)\bigr).$$ Applying this to one Fourier wave from <a href="#analytic:fourier-wave" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="analytic:fourier-wave">[analytic:fourier-wave]</a> yields exactly $$\label{analytic:transformed-jet}
 Q_n^{-1}\lambda_k(t)e^{i\pi\zeta_{k,v}(t)n}
       \bigl(-1,D_n-i\pi\zeta_{k,v}(t)\bigr).$$ Integration against the folded measures, followed by taking real parts, gives the full transformed contribution to the coefficient equations.

<a id="analytic:repetition-norm"></a>
There is one additional direct contribution on the tail. For $n\in L$ its positive repeated poles are precisely $m=n+36l$, $l\ge1$. All belong to $T$ because $0\le n\le21$.
